New low-genus desingularizations of three Clifford tori and related characterizations
Authors: Nikolaos Kapouleas, David Wiygul
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
For each nonnegative integer m we construct in the round three-sphere a closed embedded minimal surface of genus 48m+2548m+25 which can be interpreted as a desingularization of the union of three Clifford tori intersecting pairwise orthogonally, along a total of six great circles. Each such surface is generated, under the action of a group of symmetries, by a disc with hexagonal boundary, all of whose sides are contained in great circles. We prove a uniqueness result for this disc, and, as a corollary, we characterize these surfaces. This characterization implies that similar surfaces we constructed for sufficiently high m by gluing methods, in an earlier article, coincide with the ones here. For low m the surfaces constructed here are new. Similarly, we prove uniqueness of the generating discs for one of two families constructed by Choe and Soret (namely the surfaces they call odd) and show that these surfaces also coincide, when of sufficiently high genus, with surfaces we have constructed by gluing.

Introduction

A fundamental question is to classify the low-genus closed embedded minimal surfaces in the the round 3-sphere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^3$$\end{document}S3. The answer to this question is known only for genus zero by the uniqueness of the equatorial sphere proved by Almgren [1] using a method of Hopf, and for genus one by the uniqueness of the Clifford torus by the celebrated resolution of the Lawson conjecture by Brendle [3]. Note also that the equatorial sphere and the Cifford torus have the least area among closed embedded minimal surfaces in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^3$$\end{document}S3 by the celebrated resolution of the Willmore conjecture by Marques-Neves in [21], but no classification results are known at the moment for such surfaces of low area and genus g≥ 2g≥2.

The first such surfaces for any genus ≥ 2≥2 were discovered by Lawson [20] via a tiling construction based on solving a Plateau problem to construct a fundamental domain of the surface. Modifying this approach, Karcher-Pinkall-Sterling discovered nine more such surfaces [14], Choe-Soret discovered more such surfaces desingularizing Clifford tori intersecting along two great circles [4], and Bai-Wang-Wang seven more such surfaces [2]. Karpukhin-Kusner-McGrath-Stern found such surfaces doubling the equatorial two-sphere by a variational approach based on maximizing the normalized first eigenvalue subject to appropriate symmetry and topological constraints [15]. Min-max constructions have also been successful in proving the existence of such surfaces, although so far they only provide limited control of their geometry and topology [16–18, 23, 24]. Constructions by PDE gluing methods have been very successful but so far they have been limited to high-genus surfaces [6–8, 11–13, 25, 26].

There are many cases of such surfaces constructed differently but resembling each other. In such cases they are often expected to be identical up to congruence, but not known to be so [8, Remark 5.23]. A natural proof would be by characterizing them by properties implied by their constructions. Unfortunately characterizations are known at the moment only for the Lawson surfaces [5, 9, 19].

Motivated partially from the above we construct in this article a new family of closed embedded minimal surfaces in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^3$$\end{document}S3 which includes low-genus examples not known before. The surfaces we construct desingularize three Clifford tori intersecting along six great circles and therefore have area less than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6\pi ^2$$\end{document}6π2. Moreover we prove a characterization which implies that in the high-genus case they are identical to some of the surfaces we constructed in [11] by PDE gluing methods. We characterize similarly the surfaces in a family discovered by Choe-Soret in [4], and we conclude that they are identical to some other of the surfaces in [11]. We proceed to describe these results.

In [4] J. Choe and M. Soret constructed in the round 3-sphere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^3$$\end{document}S3 for each integer k ≥ 2k≥2 two families, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{T^o_{k,2km}\}_{m=1}^\infty $$\end{document}{Tk,2kmo}m=1∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{T^e_{k,2km}\}_{m=2}^\infty $$\end{document}{Tk,2kme}m=2∞, of closed embedded minimal surfaces which can be regarded as desingularizations of the union of a maximally symmetric configuration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{T}[k]$$\end{document}T[k] of k Clifford tori intersecting transversely along a pair of great circles at distance π /2π/2. The surfaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^o_{k,2km}$$\end{document}Tk,2kmo and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^e_{k,2km}$$\end{document}Tk,2kme both have genus 1+4(k-1)km1+4(k-1)km. Choe and Soret call these surfaces, respectively, odd and even, in view of their symmetries. The construction of the odd surfaces is reminiscent of Lawson’s tiling construction [20]; namely, Choe and Soret solve a certain Plateau problem with piecewise geodesic boundary in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^3$$\end{document}S3, and they then extend the solution to a closed embedded minimal surface by applying the group generated by the reflections through the great circles containing the boundary of the solution. The even surfaces are instead constructed by applying a different group of symmetries to the solution to a partially free boundary value problem, subject to a certain symmetry condition, but we will not discuss them further in this article.

In [11] we applied gluing methods to construct many closed embedded minimal surfaces admitting the same interpretation (as desingularizations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigcup \textbf{T}[k]$$\end{document}⋃T[k]) and also examples which desingularize rather the union of the collection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{T}[k,1]$$\end{document}T[k,1] consisting of the Clifford tori in the preceding collection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{T}[k]$$\end{document}T[k] together with the additional Clifford torus equidistant from the original two circles of intersection. (This last union \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigcup \textbf{T}[k,1]$$\end{document}⋃T[k,1] then has 2k+22k+2 great circles of intersection.) Since the surfaces of [11] are constructed by gluing, all have high genus.

In the present article we adapt the Plateau tiling method used in [20] and, for the odd surfaces, in [4] to construct further desingularizations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigcup \textbf{T}[2,1]$$\end{document}⋃T[2,1], a union of three Clifford tori intersecting pairwise orthogonally along six great circles. We also adapt arguments from [9] and [10], there used to establish uniqueness and a certain graphicality of the Plateau solutions generating the Lawson surfaces (which can be regarded as desingularizations of intersecting spheres), in order to establish analogous results, namely Theorem 3.25, for solutions to Plateau problems generating desingularizations of intersecting Clifford tori. As one application of Theorem 3.25 we obtain a characterization of the new desingularizations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigcup \textbf{T}[2,1]$$\end{document}⋃T[2,1] which in particular suffices to confirm that they agree, when of sufficiently high genus, with the members of a certain subfamily of the surfaces we constructed in [11].

Theorem 1.1

For each integer m ≥ 0m≥0 there exists in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^3$$\end{document}S3 a unique closed embedded minimal surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_m$$\end{document}Mm whose intersection with the interior of the prism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{00}$$\end{document}Ω00 (defined below 4.22 and described in 4.21) is nonempty and satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial (M_m \cap \Omega _{00}) = \Omega _{00} \cap \bigcup (\mathbf {\Gamma }\cup \textbf{L}^{4+8m}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂(Mm∩Ω00)=Ω00∩⋃(Γ∪L4+8m)(where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Gamma }$$\end{document}Γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}^{4+8m}$$\end{document}L4+8m are collections of great circles specified in 4.7-4.8 and 4.17). Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_m$$\end{document}Mm has genus 25+48m25+48m, contains every great circle in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Gamma }\cup \textbf{L}^{4+8m}$$\end{document}Γ∪L4+8m, and is invariant under the group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {G}^{4+8m}$$\end{document}G4+8m (defined in 4.23 and described in 4.38). Finally, in view of the uniqueness assertion, for all sufficiently large m the surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_m$$\end{document}Mm is congruent to the surface in [11] obtained (as in [11, Theorem 7.1]) from the initial surface N(2, 2m+1, 1, 1, 1, 0, 1)N(2,2m+1,1,1,1,0,1) (as defined in [11, Definition 4.13]).

Section 4 is devoted to the proof of Theorem 1.1.

As another application of Theorem 3.25, we obtain also a characterization of the odd surfaces of Choe and Soret, which in particular confirms that they agree, when of sufficiently high genus, with the members of a certain subfamily we constructed in [11].

Theorem 1.2

For all integers k ≥ 2k≥2 and m ≥ 1m≥1 there is in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^3$$\end{document}S3 a unique closed embedded minimal surface whose intersection with the interior of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{\frac{\pi }{4},\frac{\pi }{k},\frac{\pi }{2km}}$$\end{document}Ωπ4,πk,π2km (as defined in 3.7 and described in 3.8) is nonempty and has boundary the hexagon consisting of all the geodesic edges of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{\frac{\pi }{4},\frac{\pi }{k},\frac{\pi }{2km}}$$\end{document}Ωπ4,πk,π2km except the one along \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{(1)}$$\end{document}C(1); this surface is congruent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^o_{k,2km}$$\end{document}Tk,2kmo (as constructed in [4, Theorem 1]). In particular for all m sufficiently large \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^o_{k,2km}$$\end{document}Tk,2kmo is congruent to the surface in [11] obtained (as in [11, Theorem 7.1]) from the initial surface M(k, 2m, 1, 1, 0) (as defined in [11, Definition 4.13]).

The proof of Theorem 1.2 is given at the end of Sect. 3.

We denote the sets of unitary and antiunitary transformations on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}^2$$\end{document}C2 by, respectively,2.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} U(2)&:= \{ \textsf{T}: \mathbb {C}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb {C}^2 \; : \; \forall x,y \in \mathbb {C}^2 \;\; \langle \textsf{T}x, \textsf{T}y \rangle _{\mathbb {C}^2} = \langle x,y \rangle _{\mathbb {C}^2} \}, \\ \overline{U}(2)&:= \{ \textsf{T}: \mathbb {C}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb {C}^2 \; : \; \forall x,y \in \mathbb {C}^2 \;\; \langle \textsf{T}x, \textsf{T}y \rangle _{\mathbb {C}^2} = \overline{\langle x,y \rangle }_{\mathbb {C}^2} \} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U(2):={T:C2→C2:∀x,y∈C2⟨Tx,Ty⟩C2=⟨x,y⟩C2},U¯(2):={T:C2→C2:∀x,y∈C2⟨Tx,Ty⟩C2=⟨x,y⟩¯C2}(where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot ,\cdot \rangle _{\mathbb {C}^2}$$\end{document}⟨·,·⟩C2 is the standard (Hermitian) inner product on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}^2$$\end{document}C2) and we identify them as subsets of O(4) in the obvious way. Uniqueness and graphicality of solutions to the Plateau problems

Proposition 3.10

\usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{S,\Theta ,Z}$$\end{document}ΩS,Θ,Z for K is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} ,Z}) {\setminus } ( T^-_{S,\Theta ,Z} \cup P_{S,\theta ,Z} \cup T^+_{S,\Theta ,Z} ) $$\end{document}(RS,Θ,Z-∪RS,Θ,Z+)\(TS,Θ,Z-∪PS,θ,Z∪TS,Θ,Z+),(ii)The set of exit points of \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∓2d2dt2t=0f±(α±(t))=sinΘ21+2cos2Θ4>0,but \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Finally, let O be an orbit through a point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in (R^- \cup R^+) \cap (T^- \cup P \cup T^+)$$\end{document}p∈(R-∪R+)∩(T-∪P∪T+). In particular, by ii, p is an exit point for K. By v, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O \cap \Omega \ne \{p\}$$\end{document}O∩Ω≠{p}, then there is also an entry point for K on O, and then p would also have to an entry point for -K-K, violating iv and so confirming vi. □ □

Now we can prove uniqueness and K graphicality (as defined in the statement below) of the solution to a certain Plateau problem in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{S,\Theta ,Z}$$\end{document}ΩS,Θ,Z.

Theorem 3.25

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \in (0,\pi /4]$$\end{document}S∈(0,π/4], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta \in (0,\pi /2]$$\end{document}Θ∈(0,π/2], and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z \in (0,\Theta /2]$$\end{document}Z∈(0,Θ/2]. Then there is a unique connected minimal surface (with boundary) D, an embedded disc, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \subset \Omega _{S,\Theta ,Z}$$\end{document}D⊂ΩS,Θ,Z and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \partial D = (R^-_{S,\Theta ,Z} \cup R^+_{S,\Theta ,Z}) \cap ( T^-_{S,\Theta ,Z} \cup P_{S,\Theta ,Z} \cup T^+_{S,\Theta ,Z} ), \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂D=(RS,Θ,Z-∪RS,Θ,Z+)∩(TS,Θ,Z-∪PS,Θ,Z∪TS,Θ,Z+),the hexagon which is the union of the edges of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{S,\Theta ,Z}$$\end{document}ΩS,Θ,Z except \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{S,\Theta ,Z}^- \cap R_{S,\Theta ,Z}^+$$\end{document}RS,Θ,Z-∩RS,Θ,Z+, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{S,\Theta ,Z} \cap T_{S,\Theta ,Z}^+$$\end{document}PS,Θ,Z∩TS,Θ,Z+, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{S,\Theta ,Z} \cap T_{S,\Theta ,Z}^-$$\end{document}PS,Θ,Z∩TS,Θ,Z-, Furthermore, D is graphical with respect to K in the sense that no orbit of K intersects D in more than one point.

Proof

By [22, Theorem 1] there exists an embedded minimal disc \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \subset \Omega _{S,\Theta ,Z}$$\end{document}D⊂ΩS,Θ,Z with the prescribed boundary. Now suppose M is a connected minimal surface in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{S,\Theta ,Z}$$\end{document}ΩS,Θ,Z with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial M = \partial D$$\end{document}∂M=∂D. We will show, by contradiction, that no orbit of K intersects D and M at distinct points. To this end suppose then that such an orbit does exist. This assumption, together with the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{S,\Theta ,Z}$$\end{document}ΩS,Θ,Z lies in the closure of a single component of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^3 \setminus (\Sigma _{\pi /2} \cup \Sigma ^{\pi /2})$$\end{document}S3\(Σπ/2∪Σπ/2), implies the existence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t' \in \mathbb {R}\setminus 2\pi \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}t′∈R\2πZ and ε >0ϵ>0 such that the intersection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \cap \textsf{R}_{C^{\pi /2}_{\pi /2}}^t M$$\end{document}D∩RCπ/2π/2tM is nonempty for t=t't=t′ but empty either for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in (t',t'+\epsilon )$$\end{document}t∈(t′,t′+ϵ) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in (t'-\epsilon , t')$$\end{document}t∈(t′-ϵ,t′). Item vi of 3.10 ensures that the intersection, when t=t't=t′, cannot include any points in the boundary of either surface (D or the rotated M). The maximum principle then forces the surfaces to coincide, but since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t' \not \in 2\pi \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}t′∉2πZ, their boundaries, again by 3.10.vi, are actually disjoint, which contradiction establishes our claim that no orbit of K intersects D and M at distinct points.

The graphicality claim is simply the special case of this last fact when M=DM=D. To prove the uniqueness, note that it follows from the embeddedness of D, the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{S,\Theta ,Z}$$\end{document}ΩS,Θ,Z is a 3-ball, and items i and ii of 3.10 that each orbit of K intersecting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{S,\Theta ,Z}$$\end{document}ΩS,Θ,Z also intersects D, and so, if M ≠ DM≠D, there would exist an orbit of K intersecting M and D at distinct points, which contradiction completes the proof. □ □

Proof of Theorem1.2

Now Theorem 1.2 is obtained as a corollary of Theorem 3.25 as follows. Choe and Soret construct \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^o_{k,2km}$$\end{document}Tk,2kmo (in the proof of [4, Theorem 1]) by (i) solving the Plateau problem, for an embedded disc—called H in [4]—in a prism—\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1^1$$\end{document}U11 in the notation of [4]—congruent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{\pi /4,\pi /k,\pi /2km}$$\end{document}Ωπ/4,π/k,π/2km with prescribed boundary a geodesic hexagon as in Theorem 3.25 (shifted by the congruence) and then (ii) checking that H can be extended to a closed embedded surface by applying the group—\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^o$$\end{document}Go in [4]—generated by the reflections through the great circles containing the sides of the hexagon. Theorem 3.25 establishes that the solution to the preceding Plateau problem is unique, and so the surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^o_{k,2km}$$\end{document}Tk,2kmo is uniquely determined by its construction.

Similarly, from the reflection principle it is clear that any closed embedded minimal surface whose intersection with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1^1$$\end{document}U11 is nonempty and has boundary ∂ H∂H is uniquely determined by its intersection with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1^1$$\end{document}U11, but Theorem 3.25 implies that this intersection is H, and so the surface is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^o_{k,2km}$$\end{document}Tk,2kmo.

Finally, to see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^o_{k,2km}$$\end{document}Tk,2kmo agrees with the minimal surface constructed in [11, Theorem 7.1] from the initial surface M(k, 2m, 1, 1, 0) ([11, Definition 4.13]) under the assumption that m is large enough to ensure the existence of the latter surface, it suffices to confirm that, modulo congruence, the intersection of the latter surface with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1^1$$\end{document}U11 is nontrivial and has boundary ∂ H∂H. For this of course it is necessary to refer to some of the details of [11], but we explain here the key points to check.

In [11] the initial surface M(k, 2m, 1, 1, 0) is constructed explicitly and then graphically perturbed to minimality by solving the corresponding nonlinear partial differential equation, first by constructing a suitable right-inverse to the linearized operator and then by appealing to the Schauder fixed point theorem to solve the nonlinear problem. The explicit construction (see [11, 4.14] and the supporting definitions) of the initial surface M(k, 2m, 1, 1, 0) makes it clear that M(k, 2m, 1, 1, 0) (modulo congruence) not only has the desired properties that its intersection with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1^1$$\end{document}U11 is nontrivial and has boundary ∂ H∂H but moreover is invariant under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^o$$\end{document}Go.

In [11] we enforce a proper subgroup of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^o$$\end{document}Go, but it is easy to see, given the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^o$$\end{document}Go invariance of this initial surface, that in this case the larger group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^o$$\end{document}Go could be enforced. (In fact the right-inverse to the linearized problem constructed in [11, Section 6] respects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^o$$\end{document}Go, without modification.) Although in [11] we did not prove uniqueness of the minimal perturbations of the initial surfaces considered there, one could in this case simply appeal to the inverse function theorem in place of the Schauder fixed point theorem, and thereby establish uniqueness, assuming m sufficiently large. Thus we conclude that the minimal surface obtained from M(k, 2m, 1, 1, 0) is invariant under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^o$$\end{document}Go, but since it is also a small perturbation of a surface whose intersection with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1^1$$\end{document}U11 is nonempty and has boundary H, its own intersection with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_1^1$$\end{document}U11 will have these same properties, completing the proof.□ □

Desingularizations of three Clifford tori intersecting pairwise orthogonally

Initial configuration

Fix a Clifford torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}_2$$\end{document}T2 containing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{(1)}$$\end{document}C(1). Packing

For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \in \textbf{C}$$\end{document}C∈C define the closed solid torus4.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_C := \{d^{\mathbb {S}^3}_C \le \pi /8\} \quad (C \in \textbf{C}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TC:={dC3≤π/8}(C∈C).

We have the following (see Fig. 1).

Proposition 4.6

(i)For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \ne C' \in \textbf{C}$$\end{document}C≠C′∈C the intersection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_C \cap T_{C'}$$\end{document}TC∩TC′ is empty if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C'=C^\perp $$\end{document}C′=C⊥ and otherwise consists of a single great circle lying on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial T_C \cap \partial T_{C'}$$\end{document}∂TC∩∂TC′. In particular \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{T_C : \; C \in \textbf{C}\}$$\end{document}{TC:C∈C} consists of six solid tori, whose interiors are pairwise disjoint.(ii)We have the equality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigcup _{C \ne C' \in \textbf{C}} (T_C \cap T_{C'}) = \bigcup _{i=1}^3 \bigcup _{C \in \textbf{C}} (\mathbb {T}_i \cap \partial T_C), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⋃C≠C′∈C(TC∩TC′)=⋃i=13⋃C∈C(Ti∩∂TC), and this set consists of twelve great circles, the collection of which we call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Gamma }$$\end{document}Γ.

(i)For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \ne C' \in \textbf{C}$$\end{document}C≠C′∈C the intersection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_C \cap T_{C'}$$\end{document}TC∩TC′ is empty if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C'=C^\perp $$\end{document}C′=C⊥ and otherwise consists of a single great circle lying on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial T_C \cap \partial T_{C'}$$\end{document}∂TC∩∂TC′. In particular \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{T_C : \; C \in \textbf{C}\}$$\end{document}{TC:C∈C} consists of six solid tori, whose interiors are pairwise disjoint.

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \ne C' \in \textbf{C}$$\end{document}C≠C′∈C the intersection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_C \cap T_{C'}$$\end{document}TC∩TC′ is empty if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C'=C^\perp $$\end{document}C′=C⊥ and otherwise consists of a single great circle lying on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial T_C \cap \partial T_{C'}$$\end{document}∂TC∩∂TC′. In particular \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{T_C : \; C \in \textbf{C}\}$$\end{document}{TC:C∈C} consists of six solid tori, whose interiors are pairwise disjoint.

(ii)We have the equality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigcup _{C \ne C' \in \textbf{C}} (T_C \cap T_{C'}) = \bigcup _{i=1}^3 \bigcup _{C \in \textbf{C}} (\mathbb {T}_i \cap \partial T_C), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⋃C≠C′∈C(TC∩TC′)=⋃i=13⋃C∈C(Ti∩∂TC), and this set consists of twelve great circles, the collection of which we call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Gamma }$$\end{document}Γ.

We have the equality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigcup _{C \ne C' \in \textbf{C}} (T_C \cap T_{C'}) = \bigcup _{i=1}^3 \bigcup _{C \in \textbf{C}} (\mathbb {T}_i \cap \partial T_C), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⋃C≠C′∈C(TC∩TC′)=⋃i=13⋃C∈C(Ti∩∂TC), and this set consists of twelve great circles, the collection of which we call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Gamma }$$\end{document}Γ.

Proof

Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \ne C' \in \textbf{C}$$\end{document}C≠C′∈C. and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in T_C \cap T_{C'}$$\end{document}p∈TC∩TC′. Then the distance between C and C'C′ is at most π /4π/4, so in fact C'C′ must be one of the four nearest neighbors of C in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C and therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d^{\mathbb {S}^3}_C|_{C'}=\pi /4$$\end{document}dC3|C′=π/4. Moreover, p must lie on a great circle intersecting C and C'C′ orthogonally, and so p lies on the unique torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}\in \{\mathbb {T}_1,\mathbb {T}_2,\mathbb {T}_3\}$$\end{document}T∈{T1,T2,T3} containing C ∪ C'C∪C′. We have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {T}\cap \{d^{\mathbb {S}^3}_C=\pi /8\} = \mathbb {T}\cap \{d^{\mathbb {S}^3}_{C'}=\pi /8\} $$\end{document}T∩{dC3=π/8}=T∩{d′3=π/8}, and this set is a great circle. Proposition 4.11

If N is even, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \bigcup _{L \in \textbf{L}^N_{\mathbb {T}_2}} (L \cap C_{(1)}^\perp ) = \bigcup _{L \in \textbf{L}^N_{\mathbb {T}_3}} (L \cap C_{(1)}^\perp ) $$\end{document}⋃L∈L2N(L∩C(1)⊥)=⋃L∈L3N(L∩C(1)⊥) and consists of 2N equally spaced points, which we call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}^N_{C_{(1)}^\perp }$$\end{document}pC(1)⊥N.

Proof

Two circles meeting one another and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{(1)}$$\end{document}C(1) orthogonally at two points meet \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{(1)}^\perp $$\end{document}C(1)⊥ orthogonally and (collectively) at four equally spaced points. □ □

Proposition 4.16

Proof

We henceforth assume N divisible by 4. Set4.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{L}^N := \bigcup _{i=1}^3 \textbf{L}^N_{\mathbb {T}_i}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LN:=⋃i=13LiN,a collection of 3N great circles. We have4.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigcup _{L \ne L' \in \textbf{L}^N} (L \cap L') = \bigcup _{L \in \textbf{L}^N} \bigcup _{C \in \textbf{C}} L \cap C = \textbf{p}^N. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⋃L≠L′∈LN(L∩L′)=⋃L∈LN⋃C∈CL∩C=pN.Concretely,4.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{L}^N_{\mathbb {T}_1} = \left\{ \left\{ \frac{e^{j\pi {\textrm{i}}/N}}{\sqrt{2}} (e^{{\textrm{i}}s}, e^{-{\textrm{i}}s}) \right\} _{s \in \mathbb {R}} \right\} _{j=1}^N. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L1N=ejπi/N2(eis,e-is)s∈Rj=1N.

Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN has at most two orbits under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {G}^N$$\end{document}GN.

Proof

Granting that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {G}^N$$\end{document}GN preserves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN, the final claim is clear from the evident facts that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle {\underline{\textsf{R}}}_L \;: \; L \in \bigcup _{i=2}^3 \textbf{L}^N_{\mathbb {T}_i} \rangle $$\end{document}⟨R_L:L∈⋃i=23LiN⟩ acts transitively on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\omega ^N_{j\ell }\}_{j+\ell \in 2\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}{ωjℓN}j+ℓ∈2Z and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle {\underline{\textsf{R}}}_\Gamma \;: \; \Gamma \in \mathbf {\Gamma }\rangle $$\end{document}⟨R_Γ:Γ∈Γ⟩ acts transitively on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{T_C\}_{C \in \textbf{C}}$$\end{document}{TC}C∈C. To check the remaining claims it suffices to do so at the level of the generators.

First suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \in \textbf{L}^N$$\end{document}L∈LN. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{\textsf{R}}}_L$$\end{document}R_L clearly preserves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}_i$$\end{document}Ti, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}_i$$\end{document}Ti along with the corresponding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}^N_{C}$$\end{document}pCN. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{\textsf{R}}}_L$$\end{document}R_L preserves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}_i$$\end{document}Ti and each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \in \textbf{C}$$\end{document}C∈C on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}_i$$\end{document}Ti, it in fact preserves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathbb {T}_i\}_{i=1}^3$$\end{document}{Ti}i=13 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C. Since they preserve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}^N$$\end{document}pN, they also preserve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Sigma }^N$$\end{document}ΣN. Colorings

By a coloring of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN we mean a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c: \mathbf {\Omega }^N \rightarrow \{0,1\}$$\end{document}c:ΩN→{0,1}. Given any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \textbf{L}^N \cup \mathbf {\Gamma }$$\end{document}γ∈LN∪Γ, we say that a coloring c is consistent along γ γ if for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _1,\Omega _2 \in \mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}Ω1,Ω2∈ΩN sharing an edge on a circle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \mathbf {\Gamma }\cup \textbf{L}^N$$\end{document}γ∈Γ∪LN we have4.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c(\Omega _1)=c(\Omega _2) \; \Leftrightarrow \; {\underline{\textsf{R}}}_\gamma (\Omega _1)=\Omega _2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}c(Ω1)=c(Ω2)⇔R_γ(Ω1)=Ω2.(Recall from 4.21.v that exactly four members of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN meet along a given edge on a given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \mathbf {\Gamma }\cup \textbf{L}^N$$\end{document}γ∈Γ∪LN, and by 4.24 these four are exchanged in pairs under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{\textsf{R}}}_\gamma $$\end{document}R_γ.) We call c consistent if it is consistent along every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \textbf{L}^N \cup \mathbf {\Gamma }$$\end{document}γ∈LN∪Γ. Note that a consistent coloring, if one exists, is uniquely determined by its value on any single member of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN; thus, if there is one consistent coloring, then there are precisely two. Note finally that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{R}\in O(4)$$\end{document}R∈O(4) preserves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN and c is a consistent coloring of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c \circ \textsf{R}$$\end{document}c∘R is also a consistent coloring of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN.

Proposition 4.27

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \in 4\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}N∈4Z be positive. The collection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN admits a consistent coloring c if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN has precisely two orbits under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {G}^N$$\end{document}GN, namely the preimages under c of 0 and 1.

Proof

Suppose c is a consistent coloring of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN. We observed in 4.24 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Omega }^N$$\end{document}ΩN has at most two orbits under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {G}^N$$\end{document}GN, and it is clear that for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi \in \{0,1\}$$\end{document}χ∈{0,1} there is a single orbit containing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c^{-1}(\{\chi \})$$\end{document}c-1({χ}). To complete the proof in this direction (⇒ ⇒) it suffices to take any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \textbf{L}^N \cup \mathbf {\Gamma }$$\end{document}γ∈LN∪Γ and check that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{\textsf{R}}}_\gamma $$\end{document}R_γ preserves c, but \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c \circ {\underline{\textsf{R}}}_\gamma $$\end{document}c∘R_γ is a consistent coloring which agrees with c on all prisms having an edge on γ γ and therefore indeed agrees with c globally. The converse direction, which we will not need, follows even more directly from the definitions. □ □

Proposition 4.28
